# In the Morning.

---

Now I feel again that I live; the night is over; I awake. I will therefore lift the first of my thoughts to you, <term>All-Merciful One<note>original: *Allgütiger*; a traditional address for God emphasizing his infinite goodness.</note></term>! you who gave me life and watched over me while I slept. — Oh, how good you must be, you who care for the smallest of your creatures! — Good you must be, and this thought shall never leave my heart: that you are good. — How comforting it is for me, with what joy I now see the world again! Everything reminds me of your goodness, everything of your benevolence.

I am your creature! I am but dust, yet brought to life by your <term>omnipotence<note>original: *Allmacht*; refers to the unlimited power of the Creator.</note></term>; I feel a heart

<vocab>In the Morning, All-Merciful One, Life, Creatures, Goodness, Benevolence, Omnipotence</vocab>

<summary>A devotional morning reflection expressing deep gratitude to the Creator for protection during the night and the gift of a new day. The author reflects on their humble status as a "speck of dust" animated by divine power.</summary>
<keywords>morning prayer, devotion, divine goodness, omnipotence, gratitude, nature of the soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the morning meditation from page 3. The sentence begins mid-thought, following the description of the heart being animated by divine power.</meta>

...within me, that feels; a soul that opens itself to the impressions of trust and love: — To whom then should I bring the firstfruits of my feelings, if not to you, <term>incomprehensible Being<note>original: *unbegreifliches Wesen*; refers to the theological concept that God's nature exceeds the limits of human reason and understanding.</note></term>! who created me? — You are a spirit, and I a mortal; — I can never comprehend you, but I can certainly feel through your works who you are. — Hear me, All-Merciful One! hear the creature who calls to you; — you are love!

Yes, you are love — all your works proclaim your love. The rising sun proclaims your majesty, and the bliss of this morning your goodness. Today I see my father again — my wife — my children — my friends — they are healthy, and joyfully hasten back into my arms. Who cared for them, for these dear ones, while I slept? Who protected them from the dangers of the night? — You, God of goodness! and shall my heart not love you? — But what does it mean to love you? That I will reflect upon. To love you means to keep your commandments, and your commandments are love. What does the incomprehensible Being who created me demand of me? — What are the commands of the <term>Ruler of Worlds<note>original: *Beherrscher der Welten*; a grand, cosmic title for the Creator typical of 18th-century devotional literature.</note></term>? — All of nature echoes love. Love God, love yourself, love your <term>neighbor<note>original: *Nächsten*; literally "the nearest one," referring to the biblical command to love one's fellow human beings.</note></term>. I had a father, and

...I loved him; I have a friend, and my heart beats for him; I have a benefactor, and my soul is moved when I think of him; and you, God! are my father, my friend, and my benefactor, and should I not love you? — O mankind! how precious you all are to me; you are my <term>fellow brothers<note>original: *Mitbrüder*; an Enlightenment-era term emphasizing the universal brotherhood of humanity under a single Creator.</note></term>, and when I embrace you, I embrace the <term>Eternal One<note>original: *den Ewigen*; a common title for God used in both Jewish and Christian devotional texts of this period.</note></term>. On this solemn morning, Creator of all beings! I will therefore make the most solemn vows to you, that I will also live according to your most holy example. I will help the wretched, protect the oppressed, take in the orphaned, clothe the naked, heal the wounded, and lead the erring onto the right path. I will be gentle with my subordinates; affectionate with those whom nature has led into my arms; and I will regard everyone created in your image as my brother, and as a child who is dear to you.

Lord! what I say to you here, I feel in my heart! I want to be sincere with you, like a child, you who search the innermost depths of my soul: — therefore, in these first hours of the day, I also want to give you the first proofs of my love. For your sake, I forgive every one of my enemies, and promise you, Lord: that I [will do] good to everyone... <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the bottom of the page.</note>

I therefore resolve never to speak ill of my <term>neighbor<note>original: *Nächsten*; while often translated as "neighbor," in this context it refers to the broader concept of "fellow man" or "others."</note></term>; to make no unfair interpretations of his actions; not to mock him; and never to treat him poorly when he errs. I resolve to set no bad example for him, never to be unjust toward him, never to deceive or cheat him, but always to act toward him as you have commanded me. I will not be envious of him when you bless him, nor stingy when he requires my support. If I cannot help him through material means, then I will assist him with counsel and guidance; and all of this because these are your holy commandments, and because I love you.

## Human Destiny.

Great and noble is our destiny, O mankind! In the <term>chain of things<note>original: *Kette der Dinge*; this refers to the "Great Chain of Being" (*scala naturae*), a common 18th-century philosophical concept that viewed the universe as a strict hierarchical structure descending from God down to the smallest particle.</note></term> we are at the

<vocab>Love of neighbor, Commandments, Reflection, Human Destiny</vocab>

<summary>The author concludes a series of moral resolutions focused on forgiveness and selfless service to others as an expression of love for God. The text then shifts to a philosophical contemplation of the noble "destiny" or vocation of humanity within the cosmic hierarchy.</summary>
<keywords>ethics, forgiveness, neighborly love, Enlightenment philosophy, Great Chain of Being, human purpose, morality</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Great Chain of Being" from the previous page, focusing on the dual nature of humanity as both physical animals and spiritual beings capable of infinite progress toward the divine.</meta>

highest stage of created beings in this <term>physical world<note>original: *Körperwelt*; the world of matter and tangible things as opposed to the realm of spirits.</note></term>. We already border on the <term>angels<note>In the hierarchy of the Great Chain of Being, angels were the step immediately above humans.</note></term>, and the feeling of our soul already swings us upward toward the spirit, even though the heavy mass of the body still holds us back. Infinite are the capacities that lie within us; infinite are the paths of progress toward a higher <term>destiny<note>original: *Bestimmung*; can also be translated as "vocation" or "purpose," implying a preordained goal for human development.</note></term>—toward perfection—toward becoming similar to the <term>Godhead<note>original: *Gottheit*; the essence or nature of God.</note></term>. Everything proclaims to us that we are wanderers here below, and he who feels his destiny does not linger on this journey, but hurries forth on the paths that <term>Providence<note>original: *Vorsicht*; in this historical context, it refers to the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power.</note></term> has mapped out for him, and awaits the signal of the <term>Eternal One<note>original: *Ewigen*; a common Enlightenment-era epithet for God.</note></term>.

In our bodies we are animals, and in our souls we are immortal spirits; <term>sensuality<note>original: *Sinnlichkeit*; here referring to the information gathered by the senses and the physical desires of the body.</note></term> and decay are the portion of the shell that surrounds us; freedom and immortality are what our spirit awaits. The more a person clings to the physical, the more he is an animal; the more a person strives for the spiritual, the more he approaches the angel. There are, therefore, a thousand levels from the most imperfect to the most perfect, from the "animal-human" to the "spirit-human"; from the one who bridges the distance from animal to human, and from the one who makes the final step from human to angel.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection on the human condition, moving from the dual nature of man (body and soul) to the moral imperative of seeking perfection through love and virtue.</meta>

<term>Progress<note>original: *Fortgang*; implies a continuous movement or advancement toward a moral or spiritual goal.</note></term> toward higher <term>perfection<note>original: *Vollkommenheit*; a central Enlightenment concept referring to the fulfillment of one's inherent potential and moral character.</note></term> is good—true good—and true good is our <term>destiny<note>original: *Bestimmung*; also translated as "vocation" or "purpose," this refers to the specific goal for which humanity was created.</note></term>. Religion and revelation teach us to know them. To be virtuous means to form oneself more in the likeness of the <term>Godhead<note>original: *Gottheit*; the essence or nature of God.</note></term>—to approach closer to human destiny—to step forward more toward <term>unification<note>original: *Einswerdung*; the mystical or spiritual process of becoming one with the divine.</note></term>.

God is the most perfect, purest love; his highest <term>bliss<note>original: *Seligkeit*; a state of supreme spiritual happiness or "blessedness."</note></term> consists in infinitely active love; his laws are love, and our <term>calling<note>original: *Beruf*; while it can mean "profession" in modern German, here it refers to a spiritual or moral summons or life's work.</note></term> is love. Love me!—love one another! this is his commandment. Oh, if only we knew these commandments! how blessed this earth here below would be!—but darkness still removes us from the light. As soon as people love one another, the world is happy; but they do not love one another, they hate one another; they desecrate their destiny and degrade themselves to the level of a <term>ravenous beast<note>original: *reißenden Thiere*; a metaphor for humans who abandon their spiritual nature to indulge in violent, animalistic instincts.</note></term>. —But let me speak no further of this: very few people understand me; and to those who do understand me, the Godhead whispers this language into their hearts, and they have no need for the encouragement of a weak mortal.

<summary>The author argues that human destiny lies in moral progress and the imitation of God through active love, lamenting that humanity often fails this calling by choosing hatred over spiritual light.</summary>

## On the Feeling of My Existence.

I feel my existence, which is to say: I feel that I live; I feel that I am a being who is conscious of himself. My eye sees the glory of nature: the blooming fields, the green groves, the majestic forests; my ear hears the rushing of the brooks, the notes of the lark, the songs of the nightingale. I smell the aromatic flowers, feel the fragrance of the scented roses; my cheeks are receptive to the influences of the gentle west wind; the light <term>zephyr<note>original: *Zephir*; in Greek mythology, the personification of the West Wind, often used in literature to describe a soft, gentle breeze.</note></term> plays with my curls and cools my hot cheeks with its breath. Here are fruit-laden branches that give me nourishment and refreshment; I feel a pleasant delight in their taste; here the pear invites me, there the apricot, and there the cherry. I want to go, and I can go; I want to pluck the fruits, and I can pluck them; I enjoy, and I feel bliss in the enjoyment, and that which is conscious of this feeling—that is I. I am a <term>riddle to myself<note>original: *Räthsel meiner selbst*; a common Enlightenment-era philosophical theme regarding the mystery of how physical matter and conscious thought are united in one person.</note></term>, who is here now, and once was not here. I am, I feel my existence; and who placed me here? — Who gave me this magnificent <term>structure<note>original: *Bau*; likely referring to the human body as a "house" or "frame" for the soul.</note></term>?

<summary>A lyrical reflection on self-awareness where the author explores how sensory experiences—sight, sound, smell, and taste—confirm their existence and lead to deeper questions about the origin of the self.</summary>
<keywords>existence, consciousness, sensory perception, nature, self-reflection, Enlightenment philosophy, anatomy of the soul</keywords>

— Who [granted]<note>The original German "Wer die Empfänglichkeit..." is elliptical, omitting the verb. If I were here all alone — oh, how soon I would be disgusted even by the most beautiful joys of nature! But as it is, he created for me a thousand **fellow creatures**, and placed a higher feeling in my heart: to also feel joy when others feel it. 

Everything that lies around me is alive; here, a thousand colorful insects crawl upon the smallest of leaves, and all of them live, all of them feel his goodness. — There, the bird sings harmoniously in the thicket, and there the lion joyfully roars his majestic existence; there the dove coos, there the **linnet**<note>original: *Hänfling*; a small finch common in Europe, often referenced in poetry for its pleasant song.</note> flutters; here the **whitethroat**<note>original: *Grasmücke*; a type of warbler. These specific bird names emphasize the author's close observation of the local natural world.</note> hops about, singing and rejoicing in life — and I am there in the midst of it — seeing and feeling — — feeling an inner power, an impulse that tells me: *You are similar to the one who created you.* I hear a voice; it whispers to me: *Enjoy life!* and many happy sensations, never felt before, rise up in my heart. I feel love and friendship; here, nature leads me into the arms of a gentle being,

How good you are, you who made all of this so! — How good you must be! You are love — to become like you in love, that is my **calling**<note>original: *Beruf*; in this context, it refers to a spiritual or teleological vocation rather than a professional job.</note>; everything proclaims it to me: It is your law. My **sense of self**<note>original: *Selbstgefühl*; a central concept in the Enlightenment, referring to the internal awareness of one's own existence and moral standing.</note> wrote it into my heart with indelible letters; the wish to see everything around me happy is the most blessed of all wishes. Everything around us is happiness for mankind; even that which is misfortune in the world is preparation for happiness. You wanted to stir the purest of the feelings of the soul within us — to stir within us the drive for **sympathy**<note>original: *Theilnahme*; literally "taking part," this refers to the capacity for empathy and sharing in the experiences of others.</note> in joys and sufferings. To you, my children! you said, to you, to yourselves among one another I want to entrust your happiness; stretch out the arms I gave you to support one another in turn, and feel the pleasure of drying the tear in the eye of a brother. Yes, that was your language, every flower proclaims it to me; yes, only if

# Morning Song.

O how joyful is the morning,
->When the sun raises itself,<-
And the soul, free from cares,
->Pleads to the God of creation!<-
When in that gentle hour,
->Where day breaks upon the mountains,<-
My soul laments its wound<note>original: *Wunde*; in 18th-century devotional poetry, the "wound" often refers to a spiritual longing, a sense of incompleteness, or the "pain" of divine love that seeks union with the Creator.</note>
->To Him, the God of Love;<-
When it longs for refreshment,<note>original: *Labung*; a term for spiritual solace or comfort, often compared to a cooling drink for a thirsty soul.</note>
->And full of pure confidence<-
To Him, who knows all hearts,
->Speaks of the feeling of love.<-
My spirit<note>original: *Gemüthe*; refers to the seat of emotions and character, often translated as "disposition" or "inner soul."</note> feels joy,
->Everything that lives on earth,<-
Sees the image of your goodness,
->Woven into all creation.<-
Even in the little worm in the dust
->Culing itself at my feet,<-
My heart feels through pure faith
->Itself attuned to the love of God.<-

<note>Vocabulary: **Morning Song** (Morgenlied), **Creation** (Schöpfung), **Soul** (Seele), **God of Love** (Gott der Liebe), **Faith** (Glaube)</note>

<meta>This page continues the "Morning Song" (Morgenlied) from the previous page, shifting from the general observation of God in creation to the individual's personal relationship with the Divine and their internal sense of peace despite earthly suffering.</meta>

In the silver of the daffodils,
   In the morning-red of the roses,
In the springs that flow there,
   I see my living God.
Even in my whole being
   I feel His goodness;
Within myself I can read
   The calling to blessedness.<note>original: *Seligkeit*; a central term in 18th-century German theology, referring to a state of supreme happiness, spiritual peace, or eternal salvation.</note>
Full of that gentle joy
   My blood flows hopefully,
And I think, when I suffer,
   In the beyond<note>original: *Jenseits*; referring to the afterlife or the spiritual realm after death.</note> things will be well again.
Even if people plague me,
   And persecute me without guilt,
I will lament it to God, the Faithful One,
   And He then gives me patience.
Even if enemies want to kill me,
   And my blood reddens their dagger;
I will pray for them as I die,
   For You too, my God! are good.
I will build my hope,
   Father! only upon You alone,
And trust in You alone,
   For You surely help me.
You are truth, O God! and love.
   I am Your child, and You are my God!

May no hour be dark for me,
And no suffering and no death.
The sun vanishes here
Like the course of a human life,
And rises with rejuvenated joy

We are near to the Light,
When our immortal soul
Breaks through the shell of the body.

Of imposed virtue falls away,

Truth rises from the grave.

## Elevation of Sentiment
->towards the<-
### Creator.

Great God! I pray to You, give me the innocence of wisdom, for I recognize that even the angels are only wise before You insofar as their souls are innocent and pure. I recognize that I can attribute nothing to myself of the good that is in me

<note>Vocabulary: **God** (Gott), **Creator** (Schöpfer), **Soul** (Seele), **Immortality** (Unsterblichkeit), **Wisdom** (Weisheit), **Virtue** (Tugend), **Truth** (Wahrheit)</note>

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the immortality of the soul and the shedding of superficial earthly virtues, followed by a prayer seeking divine wisdom through spiritual innocence.</summary>
<keywords>afterlife, immortality, soul, spiritual purity, wisdom, angels, virtue, devotion</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the prayer "Elevation of Sentiment towards the Creator." The author is in the midst of a confession, moving from a realization of personal inadequacy toward a total reliance on divine guidance and providence.</meta>

is; but rather that I have to thank You, my Lord and God, for everything. My own work is only evil; the good that is in me is Yours, Creator and Maker! —

I pray to You, infinite light of truth! lead me on the paths of my journey<note>original: *Wanderschaft*; this refers to the "pilgrimage of life," a common metaphorical concept in 18th-century devotional literature where life is seen as a temporary journey toward a spiritual home.</note>; by You alone I wish to be led; I do not wish to be guided by my own conceit, my self-pride, and my self-love. I call to You, Lord! Lead me! I wish to love everything that You allow me to recognize as good; I wish to delight in everything that is true, for You, Lord! are goodness and truth; and I know and feel that to love the good, to will it and do it, means to love You, my God! To love the true, to will it and do it, is the love of one's neighbor — let me love You, let me love my neighbor!

With everything You gave me, I am content, because I know that I have received as much as is beneficial for me, and because You have taught me that for those who receive little, only little is beneficial. — I, as Your creature, do not know what is beneficial for me, but You alone know it, O Lord! who providently cares for all things. He who truly hopes in You and chains himself to You cannot be lost.

To you I cry out then, Father! and whoever calls to you, "Father!", you cannot cast away. I remind you of your promise. I am a weak human being, and if my child were to come to me now, pleading for mercy, I would not cast them away; and you, whose love far exceeds the love of all creatures, could you be able to abandon me? — O, whoever could think such a thing does not know you. — You are love, and what can love not do!

## God is Love.

Almighty Being, who created me, to whom I owe my existence! — I wish to reflect upon you. — You are! All of nature proclaims your existence to me. You glow in the purple<note>original: *Purpur*; in 18th-century German, this often referred to a deep, vibrant crimson or red, particularly the color of a blooming rose.</note> of the rose, and your goodness is woven into the silver of the narcissus. — I live, and I feel. — To whom do I owe this life? To whom the feeling of bliss<note>original: *Wonnegefühl*; a term describing an intense, often spiritual, state of joy or rapture.</note> that flows through my veins? To whom the gentle delight<term>original: *Wollust*; in this period, the word was used to describe intense sensory pleasure or "delight" in a general sense, rather than the modern, specifically sexualized meaning of "lust."</term> of my senses? Who gave me my eye, to see the beauties of nature? Who the ear, that hears the soft tones of the birds' harmonious songs? Who the tongue, that refreshes itself at the spring, and is revived by the fruit of the tree? Who gave me these hands, that reach out for the flowers blooming in meadows and valleys? Who gave me the powers of gentle memory, by virtue of which I [recall] the—<note>The original German text ends mid-sentence with "Ge-", likely intended to be *Gedanken* (thoughts) or *Gegenwart* (presence), continuing on the next page.</note>

## Fear of God.

---

What then is the fear of God! — Today I will set down my contemplation <term>original: *Betrachtung*; a formal spiritual reflection or meditation.</term> upon this. From my youth, as far as I can tell, I have been taught very incorrect concepts of it. Under the word "fear," one always understood a kind of aversion. I remember that I feared bad people — people who did me harm: such a fear cannot be the fear of God. God is love: and can one tremble before love? — No! The fear of God is quite a different feeling. The concern <term>original: *Besorgniß*; here meaning a tender apprehension or care born of deep affection.</term> of acting against the will of the one whom one loves—that is my fear; to fear that which is contrary to divine love—that is my holy fear—that is the fear of love. I fear you, my God! and in no other way. You are not like the great ones of the earth, who have slaves at their feet who tremble before their laws. Your relationship is the relationship of a father to his children: love and trust make the chain that binds people to you.

Forgive me, my God! that I learned to recognize you so late. It was not my fault: you were described to me quite differently than you truly are. When I was still a child, and a thunderstorm <term>original: *Gewitter*; here used to illustrate the 18th-century "pedagogy of fear" where natural disasters were used to teach divine wrath.</term> stood in the sky, they cried out to me: — Listen! the thunder roars: God is angry. — My soul, which innocently stretched its arms out toward you, trembled; I was afraid of you and hid myself; I looked at you like a human being who has his good and bad moods. Forgive me, you God of goodness! I did not know you; I judged you like a powerful ruler; I hardly dared to lift my gaze to you, and I held you to be like the great ones of the earth, whose worldly greatness makes us dizzy. Forgive me, Father! my heart was not to blame that I misjudged you for so long. Often when I sat alone in the meadows, every flower beckoned your goodness to me. The rose instilled trust in my heart, and the violet in the valley proclaimed your goodness to me. When I failed,

-se is not like an offense against a human being. This subject is important, very important; I must reflect upon it maturely.

What is it called, then, to offend God? — Transgressing your commandments. To sin is, in the general concept, to offend God. Where then does the word offense <note>original: *Beleidigung*; in German, the word literally implies "to cause sorrow" (*Leid*), which the author uses to pivot from a legal definition to an emotional one</note> come from? — It is meant to say: to inflict a sorrow; so if I transgress the commandments, I cause God some sorrow. What does that mean? — In my soul, I must not imagine you like a human being, but as what you are — as a God, as the purest love. You love us; your commandments are therefore the intentions of your love; you give your commandments to make us happy; if I then do not keep them, I act against your intentions and I inflict sorrow (if I may use this expression) upon your love. How can I make this more tangibly understandable to myself? — I will look back at myself. I love my child and therefore want everything that can make my child happy. I say now: "My child! Do not eat of this fruit; the apple is poisonous; you would harm yourself!" But the child eats it anyway and kills itself. Now it acts against the intention of my love and offends <note>here meaning "violates" or "wounds"</note> my love;

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the relationship between God and humanity, moving from the metaphor of a parent and child to the metaphor of a benevolent king and his subjects. It further develops the idea that "sin" is not an insult to a prideful God, but a rejection of the path to one's own happiness.</meta>

...in the same way, human beings act against divine love by transgressing the commandments; for your commandments, O Lord! are not commandments of power or force; but rather commandments of love, without the fulfillment of which a person cannot be happy. Let me clearly recognize this, Father of humanity!—that everything you command is for my own happiness. How worthy of love you are to me always; the more I reflect upon you, the more I recognize you. Everything you want is only for the sake of our well-being: what you command, our own feelings have already written into our hearts. Therefore your yoke is sweet, and your burden is light <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 11:30: "For my yoke is easy and my burden is light."</note>; and when I then think back on your love, on the love of the God-man <term>God-man: (original: *Gottmensch*) A theological term referring to Jesus Christ, expressing the belief that he is simultaneously fully divine and fully human.</term> who gave his life for our salvation, in what light does the transgression of your commandments then appear to me? — I will make the matter tangibly understandable to myself through an example.

There was a King; he loved his subjects like his children; their happiness alone was his concern; his laws were laws of love; he made his land into a paradise for them. "Enjoy this happiness," he said to them; "but do not stray beyond the borders; for on the other side lives a barbarian king, who would [throw] you into

<note>The text ends mid-sentence as the author prepares to describe the consequences of leaving the benevolent king's protection.</note>

**divine love, commandments, God-man, salvation, king, subjects, laws of love, paradise**

<summary>The author argues that God's laws are not displays of power but instructions for human joy, introducing a parable of a loving king to illustrate how sin leads a person away from safety.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, commandments, happiness, God-man, salvation, parable, benevolence, moral law</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegory of the benevolent King and the barbarian ruler. The previous page ended with the King warning his subjects not to cross the border, where a barbarian king would throw them into captivity.</meta>

...put you in chains and condemn you to slavery. But the subjects did not obey the good King; they crossed the borders, were captured, and were thrown into horrific dungeons. The King still wanted to save his subjects, for his love remained constant. He sent his only son and offered him as a hostage; but even more was demanded; they demanded his life. "I am willing to lose this too," said the son, "if my people—those my father loves—are free once more." During this time, despair reigned in the dungeon: the prisoners were driven to murder one another. "Do not murder one another!" wrote the good King. "Preserve yourselves! This is the law I give to you; I desire only your well-being. Consider that my own son gives his life for you; therefore, do not let this sacrifice of love be in vain!" — But despite this, they transgressed the commandment of love and murdered themselves. Now the day of redemption <term>Redemption: (original: *Erlösung*) The theological concept of being "bought back" or saved from a state of suffering or sin through a price paid by another.</term> arrives; the King’s son dies for the ungrateful; he believes he can save them through his blood; and yet they frustrated his ultimate purpose and insulted his love.

This image seems to me to express the nature of the offense against God <term>Offense against God: (original: *Beleidigung Gottes*) Often translated as "sin" or "affront," here used to describe how human self-destruction grieves the Creator rather than wounding His pride.</term> that we commit against the Deity by transgressing His commandments.

<summary>This page concludes an allegory where a King's son dies to save subjects who are destroying themselves in a foreign dungeon. The author uses this to argue that "offending God" through sin is actually a tragic rejection of His efforts to ensure our own well-being.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, King’s son, sacrifice of love, redemption, offense against God, commandments, human self-destruction</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's reflection on the preceding allegory. Having established that God's laws are intended for human well-being, the author now pivots to a personal meditation on the nature of divine love and the human "calling" to emulate it.</meta>

Yes! It is so; — Oh God! ### Insight

I have often acted contrary to the intentions of divine love; I must therefore recognize my purpose — recognize the calling <term>Calling: (original: *Beruf*) Often translated as "vocation," referring to the specific role or duty for which a person was created.</term> of my existence. God is love; my purpose is to become like divine love: — my calling is therefore love; and I can never repeat it enough to my heart. Love! Love! Love! — Love God! Love your neighbor as yourself! But what does this mean? — the gentle feelings that the Deity placed in your heart for your preservation and for your happiness, spread those also through your activity to your fellow creatures; consider them your equals; what is therefore good for you, spread also to others! what

<note>Vocabulary list from the original German: God, commandments, love, soul, insight, purpose, calling, existence, love for one's neighbor, deity, fellow creatures</note>

<summary>The author concludes that God's commandments are expressions of love and that transgressing them only harms the self. He defines the ultimate human purpose as "becoming like divine love" by extending the same care for one's own happiness to all fellow creatures.</summary>

<keywords>divine love, commandments, vocation, purpose of existence, neighborly love, empathy, morality, Deity</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 31 regarding the Golden Rule ("What pains you..."). The author transitions from the concept of innate law to the practical application of universal love as the path to divine proximity.</meta>

pains you, do not do that to others as well. — God placed these laws into my heart; thus I carry the lawbook around with me; I feel daily what is right and what is not right. <note>The author refers to the "lawbook in the heart," a common Enlightenment and theological concept suggesting that morality is innate—often called Natural Law—and accessible to everyone through conscience, regardless of their education.</note> I therefore have no need for great scholarship, and can do without libraries in order to become a good person. This is also my purpose: to become a good person. — Yes, let this be my first endeavor, and let my goal be: to become the best possible person. How can this be achieved? What leads me toward being a good person? — What leads toward being the best? — Love? — The good person is one who loves humanity; the best is one who loves them to the highest degree. Such a person draws nearer to the Deity, and thus stands at a higher level of approximation. Let my resolution therefore be: I will love humanity. —

Humanity? Meaning all people without distinction, without regard for climate or part of the world, <note>original: "Klima und Welttheil" (climate and part of the world). In the 18th century, "climate" was frequently used to categorize cultural and physical differences between peoples; the author is asserting a universal humanism that transcends these geographic and environmental divisions.</note> without regard for religion, without regard for circumstances.

All people! — Note this well, my heart; you must love all people, and thus even your enemies.

What does love do? — It cares for the well-being of the beloved object; it is gentle, it is sympathetic, merciful, it forgives, it is not self-interested, and acts without ulterior motive — all for the sake of love itself.

<note>Vocabulary from the original German: God, Lawbook, Scholarship, Purpose, Love, Deity, Religion, Enemies, Mercy</note>

<summary>The author argues that morality is an innate "lawbook" in the heart, making formal education unnecessary for virtue. He concludes that the highest human calling is the practice of universal love, which must extend to all people regardless of their background or even their enmity.</summary>
<keywords>conscience, natural law, universal love, human purpose, religious tolerance, Enlightenment humanism, enemies, mercy</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of love from page 32, specifically exploring how self-interest competes with the ideal of universal love.</meta>

What could now set limits to my love? — My self-love. <note>original: "Selbstliebe." In the 18th-century context, "self-love" often refers to egoism or self-interest, which was seen as the primary obstacle to "pure" or disinterested love for others.</note> — The height of pure love therefore corresponds to the decrease of my self-love. The less the act of my love is connected to my self-love, the purer the love is, and the higher the degree of the lover.

Therefore, in my actions toward my neighbor, my self-love must take a back seat:

I must therefore love God for the sake of God.

My neighbor for his own sake, and myself only insofar as to reach the ultimate purpose of the Deity, for which it created me, in order to preserve me for the good of humanity.

This is the <term>measure of love</term> <note>original: "Maßstab der Liebe." This refers to the standard or yardstick by which the quality of one's moral intentions is judged.</note>. Is it then perhaps hard to lead a life that guides us to heaven? — O no! it is not hard.

To renounce all riches, all positions of honor, to live constantly in contemplation, to always castigate oneself, to lie prostrate on the ground — is this the holy life that the Creator demands of us? — No, we are created for activity, not for dead contemplations. <note>The author here rejects the medieval ascetic ideal of the "monastic" life—characterized by self-punishment and withdrawal from the world—in favor of an active, socially useful "Enlightened" faith.</note> — O Lord! teach me to walk Thy ways.

When I contemplate human life, I find it to be threefold: the spiritual life, the moral

<summary>The author argues that true love requires the suppression of self-interest (self-love) and rejects asceticism in favor of a life of active service to humanity. He begins to categorize human existence into spiritual, moral, and (presumably on the next page) physical dimensions.</summary>
<keywords>self-love, pure love, neighborly love, Deity, asceticism vs activity, spiritual life, moral life</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s definition of the "threefold life" begun on page 33. It completes the transition from the "moral" life to the "civic" life and shifts the focus toward the necessity of acting with pure intentions rather than for social status or personal gain.</meta>

life, the civic life. — It belongs to the spiritual and moral life to will what is good, and to the civic life to do what is good.

The will of my God is that I will and do the good; thus, willing must be connected with doing; merely to recognize the good and not put it into practice does not mean fulfilling <term>human duty</term> <note>original: "Menschenpflicht." A central concept in Enlightenment ethics, referring to the universal moral obligations one holds toward others as a member of the human race.</note>.

The person who lives according to the laws of love is the one who recognizes what is right and puts that recognition into practice. So it is; yes, my God! Therefore You say, faith without works is dead. <note>original: "der Glaube, ohne die Werke, ist todt." A reference to James 2:26, emphasizing that religious belief is meaningless unless it results in charitable actions.</note>

However, it is not yet enough for me to recognize my duties and to fulfill them: I must also fulfill them from a <term>pure intention</term> <note>original: "reiner Absicht." This implies doing the right thing solely because it is right, rather than for any external reward or social recognition.</note>; not for the sake of the world, not out of self-love, or because of the advantages that this fulfillment might perhaps provide me; or because of the praise of the world, which it would bestow upon me as a good person: no! I do not want to fulfill them for that reason; that would be acting ignobly; but rather because of You, my God: You who are love, and want us to become like You through love. I will therefore continue to be good, even if the world should deem me evil; I will love, even if I should nowhere find a heart that [loves] me in return...

<gloss>civic life, moral life, will of God, human duty, laws of love, faith and works, pure intention, self-love</gloss>

<summary>The author concludes his definition of the threefold life by arguing that civic life requires active goodness. He emphasizes that moral duties must be performed with pure intentions for God's sake, rather than for worldly praise or personal benefit.</summary>
<keywords>civic life, moral action, pure intention, faith and works, Enlightenment ethics, self-love, human duty</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the prayer and resolution regarding "pure intention" from page 34. It completes the section on universal love and begins a new meditation on trust in God during times of hardship.</meta>

...loves me in return. <note>The sentence completes the thought from the previous page: "I will love, even if I should [not be] loved in return."</note> I will support the helpless, as much as it is in my power, even if he should reward me with ingratitude. My feeling for humanity shall be true sentiment, not sentimentality <note>original: "Empfindeley." A term frequently used in the late 18th century to criticize shallow, performative, or overly-indulgent emotionalism, as opposed to "Empfindung," which was seen as sincere and morally grounded.</note>; I do not want to give to the poor so that he might become my praiser; I do not want to support the wretched so that he might depart from my sight! and so that his wailing no longer bothers me; I do not want to be charitable because my heart cannot bear the tear of misery <note>The author argues that true charity is a rational, divine duty, rather than a mere emotional reflex to end one's own discomfort at seeing someone else suffer.</note>; but rather out of love for You, infinite Divinity! You who gave all people to me as brothers. Let this be my solemn resolution, O God of love! Through Your goodness, let this resolution come to its fruition, Amen.

### Concerning the
# True and the Good.

---

**W**hen I look around, my God! and contemplate the beautiful creation; when I consider your wise arrangements, everything calls out to me that truth and goodness are the pillars upon which heaven and earth rest.

It is therefore necessary, O Lord! that I know what is true and good, and upon this important subject I wish to reflect today.

Truth and goodness are you alone; and true and good is only that which you are. Love within knowledge <note>original: "Erkenntniß" — this refers to a deep, spiritual perception or recognition rather than mere factual learning.</note> is the Good, and love in practice <note>original: "Ausübung" — the active application or exercise of one's beliefs.</note> is the True.

Truth and goodness must be united; for truth is an object of knowledge, and goodness an object of the will: — and what would knowledge be without the will?

The understanding and the wisdom of your angels, O Lord! arise through the union of the True with the Good; without this union, there is only error and falsehood <note>The author is likely influenced by Enlightenment-era theology or mysticism, which often posited that intellectual "Truth" is useless or "false" unless paired with a "Good" heart/will.</note>.

<vocab>God, Creation, Truth, Goodness, Knowledge, Will, Understanding, Wisdom, Angels</vocab>

<summary>This page begins a meditation on the metaphysical qualities of Truth and Goodness, defining them as the pillars of creation and the essential attributes of God. It argues that wisdom only arises when intellectual knowledge is united with a good will.</summary>
<keywords>Truth, Goodness, Creation, Divine Wisdom, Will, Understanding, Angels, Knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical prayer from the previous page, moving from the definitions of Truth and Goodness to a personal petition. The author transitions from describing these concepts as "pillars of heaven" to asking for their internal realization within the human soul.</meta>

Truth, O God! You are thus, and true is everything that You are; if I therefore seek truth, I must seek You, and become like You.

You gave me, my God! will and understanding — the understanding, in order to perceive <note>original: "erkennen" — refers to the act of spiritual recognition or gaining insight</note>; the will, in order to desire that which I have perceived. You, my God! are Goodness; everything that shares in Your Being is good, and everything is true that approaches the exercise of this goodness. When I recognize Your goodness, and this recognition passes into my will, then Your goodness becomes visible through me, and my action is true.

Let me therefore realize, my God! that I must unite the Good with the True; and give me Your wisdom, so that I may have understanding and will; and let me not be like the wise ones of the world, who instead of understanding have only science <note>original: "Wissenschaft" — in this 18th/19th-century context, it refers to academic or factual learning devoid of spiritual insight</note>, and instead of will have only desire <note>original: "Begierde" — often implying worldly or carnal cravings</note>; fashion my will into a vessel for the Good, and my understanding into a vessel for the True. Amen.

## Knowledge of the Eternal.

### Human Vocation and Human Happiness.

When I reflect upon myself, my God! I feel that I am both an outer and an inner human being; I have a body and a soul; I am an animal-man <note>original: "Thiermensch" — referring to the physical, biological, and sensory nature shared with animals</note> and a spirit-man <note>original: "Geistmensch" — referring to the rational, moral, and divine-seeking aspect of humanity</note>. The outer is necessary for my present state as a human; the inner leads me toward my future state as a spirit. Let me, O Infinite One! contemplate this truth.

Senses and body connect me with the world; spirit and soul with You, O God! I thus recognize my calling; I am not here, like the animal, merely for sensory enjoyment; my destiny <term>Bestimmung: a central concept in Enlightenment-era German philosophy referring to the ultimate purpose or goal of a human being's existence</term> is higher on the ladder of progress.

You are Goodness; and the activity of goodness is love; — my destiny is to become like You; therefore my calling is to love, just as You love.

Your love is pure; so let my love also be pure, like Yours. I must therefore love the Good for the sake of the Good, and the True for the sake of 

<note>The sentence ends abruptly here as the page concludes; the author is transitioning into a discussion on the disinterested love of Truth.</note>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection from page 46, completing the sentence regarding the motivation for loving truth and goodness.</meta>

the truth; not out of calculation, nor because of the advantages that accompany what is good; not out of vanity or the thirst for glory, but simply because the good is good, and the truth is true. What, then, can hinder me from this pure love? Nothing but my self-love. I wish, therefore, to learn to know this self-love, so that I may challenge and combat it, that it may not hinder me on the path that leads to You, O All-Good One! <note>original: "Allgütiger" — a common Enlightenment-era epithet for God, emphasizing infinite benevolence.</note>

I love myself when I wish good only for myself and not for others—or for them only for my own sake; I love myself when I do good for the sake of honor, for fame, or for the sake of the world’s applause.

I am caught in self-love, then, when in all that I think and do, I look not to my neighbor, nor to the common good <note>original: "allgemeine Wohl" — the collective well-being of society, a key concept in 18th-century ethics</note>, nor to the Lord, but only to myself and those who are mine; I love myself when I seek only to satisfy my passions, without regard for the harmful consequences they cause my fellow human beings. This self-love is the opposite of pure love: for its share consists of what is evil and false, just as the share of true love is what is true and good.

# Prayers for the Service.

### Upon Entering the Temple of God.

My God! As I enter into your temple, give me the insight <note>original: *Erkenntniß*</note> that I may worship you in spirit and in truth; instill in me pure concepts of the majesty of your being, so that my heart does not look upon you as a mere human, like the great figures of the earth whose greatness consists only of outward appearance. Grant me to know that innocence of conduct and a pure heart are the first offerings that you demand of us, and that gold and silver have no value in your eyes. # Preparatory Prayers
### for the
# Holy Mass.

Lord! let me contemplate the importance of the holy sacrifice of the Mass <note>The "sacrifice of the Mass" refers to the Catholic belief that the celebration of the Eucharist is a sacramental re-presentation of Christ’s sacrifice on the Calvary.</note>; — contemplate the greatness of your love! —

->The priest approaches the altar.<-

You, the holiest of the holy! Hidden in the cloak of humanity <note>A reference to the Incarnation, the theological concept of God taking on a human body and nature.</note>! Purest virtue! Example of purest love! — You wandered upon the earth to teach us true happiness. Gently you spread your light over the darkness, but humanity did not recognize you; they hated the one who loved them and killed the one who wished to save them.

Redeemer of mankind! You looked past the ingratitude that awaited you. — You, even as grief and sorrow filled your loving heart, you prayed, O Holy One! to heaven.

### At the Kyrie.
<note>original: "Zum Kyrie." The Kyrie Eleison is an ancient Greek prayer meaning "Lord, have mercy," traditionally the first part of the Mass Ordinary.</note>

### At the Gloria.
<note>original: "Zum Gloria." The Gloria in Excelsis Deo is a hymn of praise beginning with the words "Glory to God in the highest."</note>

Praise and honor be to you in heaven, and peace to people on earth! Fashion my soul, O Creator of human happiness, toward peacefulness and love, and protect my heart from every ignoble inclination. Let me bring reconciliation among those who hate each other, and love among those who persecute one another. Enlighten the hearts of princes <note>The author here reflects Enlightenment-era concerns, urging rulers to govern with compassion rather than tyranny.</note>, so that they may honor humanity and spare the blood of their subjects. Let them see that the lowliest human being has as much value in your eyes as a king, and tune them to the divine harmony of your love.

### Preface <term>Preface: (Präfation) The introductory part of the Eucharistic prayer, which traditionally begins with a call to "lift up your hearts."</term>

My God! let me consider what Christian virtue is, and what it means to be a Christian. Christian virtue is a readiness <note>original: "Fertigkeit," meaning a practiced skill or an ingrained habit of the soul.</note> to think and act according to the precept and pattern of Jesus. To be a Christian, then, means to follow Christ, to imitate his pattern, to be like him in spirit and life: and this following consists in the earnest, active, and unceasing endeavor to become like him—partly in his pious and virtuous mind in general, and partly in his conduct toward God and people, toward friends and enemies, calling and business, truth and error, virtue and vice, honor and contempt, wealth and poverty, joys and sorrows, life and death, time and eternity—so that a great and noticeable similarity is found between us and Jesus, between his conduct and ours, and we

My God! let me recognize the full extent of my duties in the station in which you have placed me, and strengthen me for the performance of them; make me a blessed instrument of your benevolence <note>original: "Wohlthätigkeit"</note>, and let me realize that wherever I do good, it is you who have placed me in that position, for you alone are the Lord and Father of all that is good. Let me recognize that all people are your children, and that among this number are also those whom you have given to me. Thus you entrusted to the brother his brothers; love must therefore be my first concern, my first duty. Grant me wisdom and goodness, the ornaments of your throne; and let me become like you through love, so that I may make as many people happy as is within my power. ## Confession Prayers.

### Before Confession.

Grant me the grace to recognize, my God, what true repentance <term>Buße: a term encompassing both the internal feeling of remorse and the external act of penance or making amends</term> is. Simply confessing one's sins does not yet mean doing penance; for when a person has confessed their sins, has asked for forgiveness from a humble heart, turns away from those sins, and leads a new life of charity and faith, then they achieve true repentance. Therefore, the one who recognizes the evil within themselves, regrets it, guards against it, and asks the Lord for assistance and help—he is the one who truly does penance. Let me realize, then, my God, that the repentance of the mouth <note>referring to "lip service" or reciting a confession without genuine heart-felt change</note> is no repentance at all.

<summary>This page concludes a prayer for spiritual purity and begins a series of reflections on the nature of confession, emphasizing that true repentance requires a change of heart and life rather than just a verbal list of sins.</summary>
<keywords>self-love, creation, temple of the heart, confession, repentance, penance, charity, faith, sincerity</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Confession Prayers" section introduced on the previous page. Having established that true penance requires a change of heart, the author now provides a specific prayer to prepare for the "examination of conscience"—the process of reflecting on one's sins before the actual ritual of confession.</meta>

### For Enlightenment in the Examination of One's Conscience.

God of light! You know every depth of my heart, and all my sins stand recorded before you.

Enlighten me, so that I too may recognize them, and may hold a strict accounting <note>original: "Rechenschaft" — a formal rendering of accounts, as if presenting a spiritual ledger to a judge</note> with myself over my conduct of life until now.

I want to penetrate into my innermost self and overlook none of my transgressions <note>original: "Verbrechen" — while literally meaning "crimes," in this context it refers to serious spiritual offenses or sins</note>, in order to repent for each of them with all the contrition <term>Zerknirschung: a theological term for a deep, "crushing" sorrow for sin, derived from the Latin "contritio"</term> of which my soul is capable.

Let your grace guide me in this self-judgment, and may the Holy Spirit—whom you promised through the mouth of your Son to all who would ask you for it—come over me, so that there may be light in my soul, and I may recognize how often and how severely I have sinned against you, against my neighbor, and against myself in thoughts, words, and deeds.

### Simple and Reasonable Instruction,
### for the Examination of One's Conscience.

How have I lived during this time? How did I begin and spend each day? — Was it one of those lost for eternity? Could I conclude the whole series of my days with this one day? How has my heart stood toward God since then? — Did I think of His omnipresence? — of His goodness? — of His holy law? — Did I speak of these things gladly? — Did I pray? And how? — Did I give thanks for so much good that I enjoyed? — What have I done out of love for my God? — Did I follow His <term>promptings<note>original: *Entsprechungen*; refers to the soul responding to or harmonizing with divine grace or calls to action</note></term>?

## Resolution for <term>Amendment<note>original: *Besserung*; refers to the active process of improving one's character and turning away from past mistakes or "sins."</note></term>.

---

God of love! Often I have promised You <term>amendment<note>original: *Besserung*</note></term>, but every time I have broken my <term>vow<note>original: *Gelübde*; a solemn promise made to God</note></term> again, and left the paths of Your pure love. <term>Self-love<note>original: *Selbstliebe*; in the context of this era's devotional writing, this refers to egoism or selfishness that distances a person from spiritual duties.</note></term> and the love of the world have distanced me from You, Creator of the worlds! Let me recognize that only Your love creates my happiness, Father of mankind! I do not wish to <term>fill the measure of my sins<note>original: *das Maaß meiner Sünden nicht voll machen*; a biblical metaphor suggesting there is a limit to divine patience before judgment occurs.</note></term>, nor to abuse Your goodness any longer. My resolution is to obey You, and to live faithfully by Your commandments. Yet Lord! what are my decisions without Your grace? Indeed, I am sincere toward You; but I recognize that I am weak, and that without Your assistance I can do nothing. Support me in the struggle against my evil inclinations; and when my strength begins to waver, come to my aid; for even in the weak, You are the God of strength. You alone are the Lord! In You I will trust, and under Your guidance, walk the paths of this life. Amen.

Take and eat; this is my body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of me. This is the chalice, the new covenant in my blood, which is shed for you: drink from it, all of you. <note>These introductory lines are the "Words of Institution," the traditional words spoken by Jesus at the Last Supper, central to the Christian liturgy of the Eucharist.</note> I am the living bread that has come down from heaven; whoever eats of it will live for eternity; and the bread that I shall give is my flesh. Whoever eats my flesh and drinks my blood has eternal life; he remains in me, and I in him; for my flesh is truly food, and my blood is truly drink. <note>This section quotes the "Bread of Life" discourse from the Gospel of John, which emphasizes the necessity of the sacrament for spiritual life.</note>

<meta>This page continues the ecstatic movement of Friedrich Gottlieb Klopstock’s poetry. While the previous page marveled at the macrocosm—the stars, Orion, and the Earth as a "drop in a bucket"—this page shifts focus to the microcosm. The poet contemplates a tiny insect to explore the mysteries of life, mortality, and the potential for even the smallest creatures to possess a soul.</meta>

But you, little worm of spring,
That plays beside me in greenish-gold,
You live; and are perhaps—
Alas, not immortal!

I have gone out to worship;
And I weep? Forgive, forgive
Even this tear, to the finite being <note>original: "dem Endlichen"; the poet refers to himself as a "finite" or mortal creature, contrasting his own temporary nature with the eternity of God.</note>,
O You, who shall be!

You will reveal all doubts to me,
O You, who through the dark valley
Of death <note>A direct allusion to Psalm 23:4, "the valley of the shadow of death." Klopstock uses this biblical imagery to describe the transition to the afterlife where all mysteries are solved.</note> will lead me! I shall learn then,
Whether the little golden worm had a soul.

If you are only formed dust,
Son of May <note>original: "Sohn des Mays"; a poetic personification of the insect, suggesting it is a child of the springtime.</note>, then become
Once again fleeting dust,
Or whatever else the Eternal wills!

Pour out anew, my eye,
Tears of joy!
You, my harp,
Praise the Lord!

Wreathed again, with palms <note>Palms are traditional symbols of victory, peace, and eternal life; here they decorate the poet's harp as he concludes his song of praise.</note>
Is my harp wreathed! I sing to the Lord!

He concludes with a jubilant return to worship, accepting the mysteries of creation and praising God with "tears of joy" and a wreathed harp.</summary>

Here I stand. All around me

Nameless One, You!
Created it!

Breezes that blow around me, and breathe
Soft cooling upon my glowing face,
You, wonderful breezes,
Were sent by the Lord! The Infinite One!

But now they become still; they scarcely breathe.
The morning sun becomes sultry! <note>original: "schwül"; the air becomes heavy and oppressive, signaling the approach of a thunderstorm.</note>
Clouds stream upward!
He who comes is visible, the Eternal One!

Now they soar, they rush, the winds swirl
How the forest bows! How the river rises!
Visible, as You can be to mortals,
Yes, that is what You are, visible, Infinite One!

The forest bends, the river flees, and I—
Do I not fall upon my face?
Lord! Lord! God! Merciful and gracious! <note>A liturgical and biblical reference, echoing the revelation of God’s name to Moses in Exodus 34:6.</note>
You who are near! Have mercy on me!

**Key Vocabulary:**
**Omnipotence** <term>Allmacht</term>, **Creation** <term>Schöpfung</term>, **The Infinite One** <term>Unendliche</term>, **The Eternal One** <term>Ewige</term>, **Awe** <term>Ehrfurcht</term>, **God** <term>Gott</term>

<summary>In this dramatic finale to the poem, the quiet contemplation of nature erupts into a violent spring storm where the poet senses the literal presence of God. The text moves from gentle breezes to a state of ecstatic worship as the poet witnesses the "Eternal One" manifest through the power of the wind and clouds.</summary>

Are you angry, Lord,
Because night is your garment?
This night is a blessing to the earth!
Father, you are not angry!

It comes to pour out refreshment
Upon the strengthening stalk! <note>Referring to the stalks of grain in the fields, which are nourished by the rain.</note>
Upon the heart-gladdening grape!
Father! you are not angry!

Everything is silent before you, you who are near!
All around, everything is silent!
Even the little worm, covered in gold, takes notice! <note>A reference back to the "rose chafer" beetle mentioned earlier in the poem; the poet wonders if even the smallest insect possesses a soul capable of sensing God.</note>

->bolt?<- <note>original: "Strahl"; used here to describe a lightning strike as a "witness" of God's presence.</note>

The world-shaking thunder of the Lord?

### his Friend and Benefactor <note>In the 18th century, a "benefactor" (Wohlthäter) was often a patron or someone of higher social standing who provided financial or moral support. The language of "friendship" during this era was deeply emotional and often used to describe spiritual bonds.</note>

> Lord! I wish to remember the loved ones who are beyond the grave. Death closed their eyes; they are no longer here for me. Forgive me, Lord! if human feeling <note>original: "Menschengefühl." They are with You; where could they be better off, Father? — I pray for my brothers, who [the sentence continues on the next page]...

### Thanks for Immortality.

---

O you, the highest of all blisses! overflow my whole soul with your fire: I want to think of the Eternal One. — You were, you are, and you will be. How shall I name you? My trembling lip falls silent. How shall I interpret you? Even my soul stands still. No, my intellect cannot reach you. Father! Father! Thus shall my lips stammer your name, my soul think of you, my heart feel you — — — Father! Father! Father! — O fall down and worship, you people! He is the Father of us all. Immerse yourself in contemplation, my soul! Shudder, heart, with joy — you were created for immortality <note>original: "Unsterblichkeit." In the 18th and 19th centuries, the "certainty of immortality" was a central theme of religious life, often used to provide comfort against the high mortality rates of the era.</note>. Not in the rolling thunder, not in the roaring storm, but in the gentle rustling of the west wind <note>original: "Westes." Refers to the Zephyr or west wind, traditionally associated with spring and the breath of life.</note>, all of nature announces your continued existence to you. What is happening to me! — Am I already down at the grave, or already above the grave? Have I already taken the heavenly flight? O words of eternal life! There I sit now at the first ray of morning

# Prayer of a Writer.

---

God of Love! All knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschaft." In the 18th and 19th centuries, this term referred broadly to scholarship, learning, and the systematic pursuit of truth rather than just the natural sciences.</note> and wisdom come from you. I thank you, Supreme Being! for the talents you gave me; let me use these for the benefit of my fellow man. Since you gave me the ability to write, let me never forget that this ability is your gift, and that I therefore have no cause to take pride in it. Guard me from pride, which is so detrimental to true scholarship, and preserve my soul from the spirit of dogmatism <note>original: "Rechthaberei." The stubborn insistence on being right regardless of the truth.</note> and literary quarrelsomeness. Since your grace has placed me in a position to gather much knowledge, guard me so that I do not neglect my heart while I occupy myself with the cultivation <note>original: "Bildung." A central German concept referring to the development of the mind, soul, and character into a harmonious whole.</note> of my spirit. Do not let me become a sullen, quarrelsome recluse <term>Stubensitzer: literally a "room-sitter"; a derogatory term for a scholar who stays indoors and loses touch with the social world</term> who seeks no company except for the moth that devours his papers. Let the knowledge you gave me sprout for the welfare of humanity; and give me the grace that I [possess] gentle feelings

<note>The text ends mid-sentence, suggesting the prayer continues on the following page.</note>

Prayer, Writer, Knowledge/Scholarship, Wisdom, Talents, Pride, Dogmatism, Quarrelsomeness, Cultivation/Education, Reclusive Scholar.

<summary>A devotional prayer in which a writer asks God for humility and the grace to use their intellectual talents for the benefit of humanity rather than for personal ego. The text warns against the isolation and bitterness that can sometimes afflict the life of a scholar.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Prayer of a Writer" from the previous page. into the hearts of men, my brothers, and lead them onto the paths of the good and the true. Guide my heart, God of gentleness <note>original: "Gott der Sanftmuth." Enlighten my understanding, and guide my pen, so that I write down nothing that is contrary to your holy will.

An older term for a translator or spokesperson, suggesting the writer is merely a vessel for divine truth.</note> of your love. Give emphasis to my words and power to my style, so that I may move the hearts of men and lead them on gentle paths to true happiness.

Bless my works; and if I gain by them, let me apply this gain to brotherly love. A state of spiritual salvation or supreme heavenly happiness.</note>. Amen.

# Prayer
### of a man who has business at court.

You great, mighty God—you King of kings! Lord of lords!—Your will ordained <note>original: "wollte es so." Literally "willed it so," reflecting the 18th-century belief in divine providence directing one's social position.</note> that I should live at court; and your holy will granted me the station <term>Stand: refers to one’s social rank or "estate" in life, which was viewed as a divinely appointed duty rather than just a job</term> in which I live; give me, therefore, your grace, so that I may recognize the duties of my station and fulfill them faithfully. Let me do as much good as is possible in the position you gave me, and let me not be blinded by the glitter <note>original: "Scheine." Refers to the superficial splendor, vanity, and deceptive illusions typical of royal court life.</note> of the court and the opinions of men. Give me a sincere heart that loves the Sovereign <note>original: "Fürsten." Can refer to a Prince, Duke, or any ruling monarch.</note> and the Fatherland without self-interest. Let me always attend to what is best without ulterior motives, and strengthen my courage in my labors. Guard my heart so that it is not blinded by money, positions of honor, and false ambition; rather, enlighten me so that I always see that only that which deserves approval in your eyes is truly great.

<vocab>Prayer, Court, Station, Duties, Sovereign, Fatherland, Self-interest, Ambition</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a prayer for a public official or courtier, asking for the integrity to serve the state and monarch faithfully while resisting the corrupting influences of wealth and status.</summary>
<keywords>court life, public service, integrity, social station, divine providence, ambition, patriotism</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous "Prayer of a Writer" to a new prayer intended for an official or courtier. It continues the theme of seeking divine guidance to maintain moral integrity within one's specific professional and social "station."</meta>

# Prayer
### of a man who has business at the Court.

You great, powerful God—you King of Kings! Lord of Lords!—Your will ordained that I should live at the court; and your holy will gave me the station <term>Stand: social rank or profession, which in this period was often viewed as a divinely appointed place in the social hierarchy</term> in which I live; give me therefore your grace, so that I may recognize the duties of my station and fulfill them faithfully. Let me do as much good as is possible in the position you gave me, and let me not be blinded by the glitter <note>original: "Scheine." This refers to the superficial luster, vanity, or deceptive appearances common in high-society court life.</note> of the court and the opinions of men. Give me a sincere heart that loves the prince and the fatherland without self-interest. Let me always pursue what is best without [hidden] motives <note>original: "ohne Absicht." In this context, it suggests acting for the common good rather than for personal gain or through political maneuvering.</note>, and strengthen my courage in my labors. Guard my heart so that it is not blinded by money, positions of honor, and false ambition; but enlighten me, so that I may always realize that only that which deserves approval in your eyes is truly great.

<vocab>Prayer, Court, Station/Rank, Duties, Prince, Fatherland, Self-interest, Ambition</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a prayer for a courtier or government official, asking for the strength to resist the corrupting influences of power, money, and vanity. It emphasizes the importance of serving the sovereign and the country with a sincere and selfless heart.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, court life, public service, integrity, ambition, duty, social station, patriotism</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the prayer of an official or courtier, focusing on the specific moral hazards of court life such as flattery, corruption, and the pressure to conform to social expectations rather than ethical standards.</meta>

Protect me from the fear of men <note>original: "Menschenfurcht." This refers to a common theological concern of the era: prioritizing human approval and social standing over divine law or one's own conscience.</note>, which is so common at courts; and from false politics <note>original: "Politik." In this context, it refers to the deceptive maneuvering, craftiness, and cunning intrigue typical of courtly power struggles.</note>, which in the eyes of the Lord is the most abominable crime. Let me speak the truth with the strength of a man, but in such a way that my love for the truth does not become a blind passion <note>original: "Leidenschaft." This suggests a warning against becoming a hotheaded zealot whose delivery of the truth is unnecessarily offensive or aggressive.</note>, causing me to offend others. Sustain my soul, so that it does not bow before the court flatterers, and that I do not become a base bootlicker <note>original: "Speichellecker." Literally "spittle-licker," a highly derogatory term for a sycophant or someone who performs demeaning acts of flattery for personal gain.</note> of false greatness.

Let me walk the straight path; and when storms gather over me, preserve me even in the tempest. Grant me the grace that my soul may always remain constant; that love for my fatherland and devotion to my sovereign <term>Fürst: A Prince or ruling sovereign; the term implies a personal bond of loyalty between the official and the monarch.</term> may never depart from my heart; that I never renounce justice, nor virtue; and even if the very globe should shatter over me, let me remember that you are the Lord, and that your hand will still protect me even among the ruins.

# Prayer
## of a Married Woman.

My God! You gave me my husband as the companion of my life, the partner of my fate, and the companion of my days on this journey of life. Let me consider, my God! that it is he who maintains me, so that I may never cloud his days, or be ungrateful toward the maintainer of my life. Grant me that, through the cheerfulness of my spirit <note>original: "Gemüthes," referring to one's emotional and spiritual disposition</note> and through tender love, I may make the days of his life pleasant; that I may be his partner in the fates of life, and his faithful companion in good times and bad.

Preserve me from the excesses of those women who do not know how to value the worth of a husband—

### Prayer
### in Tribulations and Affliction.

Lord! Misfortune and trouble threaten me; but I do not grumble against you! You are always the God of love—the rock upon which I place the structure of my hope. You will know why all this happens, and provide for my best interests. Lord! You love me, this is a certainty; you love all people, and this thought shall be my strength. Misfortune shall succumb to me, and not I to misfortune; I will fight the hardships <term>Mühseligkeiten: the heavy toils and burdens of earthly life</term> of this life, and not grant these hardships victory over my heart through my timidity <term>Zaghaftigkeit: a lack of courage or a state of being faint-hearted</term>. I will think that the things in the world, by you, my God! are subordinate to me, and not I to the things. I am your creature, the creature of God, the Mighty. Only lifeless things and dead corpses allow themselves to be carried away by the current; the living person resists, and knows how to fight with the waves.

### Prayer during a Thunderstorm.

My God! You are also the God of love in the storm, and the God of goodness in the thunderstorm. When winds roar, the thunder rolls, lightning glows, when the earth trembles, and abysses open up; so I will stand there without fear, and think: You are the God of love; and nothing shall happen to me. All is good that your hand has made! Everything aims for the well-being of the whole, for divine harmony; everything lives in nature; there is no death. — Only change, only a transition to new life. Do you wish for the lightning to strike me? Here I stand; I am ready. Let it rob me of the garment of mortality <term>Kleid der Sterblichkeit: a poetic metaphor common in this era for the physical human body, which "clothes" the eternal soul</term>: what can it do to my immortal soul? Let it release me from the bonds of the flesh: and my spirit will find freedom with you. Lord! I worship you in the storm, amidst the roaring of the

The author expresses a lack of fear toward death, describing it merely as a transition to a new life and a release from the physical body.</summary>

God of love! if the hail devastates the fields of my neighbor, if the lightning sets his hut on fire, let me not think, my God! that my neighbor has deserved these misfortunes; let me never judge the unfortunate with uncharitableness; let never such a thought desecrate my love. You have ordered everything well; your providences <term>Fügungen: a term describing the way God "arranges" or "weaves" the events of life, often translated as "providence" or "divine decrees"</term> are providences of love; it is not punishment that struck my neighbor; this accident is a test of love for me. You wish for me to take into my hut the one who no longer has one; and that I share my fruits with the one whose own have been devastated by hail; this is your will; everything is a disposition toward divine union <term>Einswerdung: a mystical concept referring to the process of the human soul becoming one with the Divine will</term>. Lord! let me worship you in the thunder and in the storm; for you are everywhere the God of love. Amen.

### Prayer
### after receiving a victory.

My God! we have received the victory over our enemies; everyone is joyful and strikes up songs of jubilation. I, my God! will however bow myself deep to the earth, worship your decrees <term>Rathschlüsse: the divine plans or counsels of God that govern human history</term>, and ask you that you may attune the hearts of men to gentler sentiments; so that they might decide their disputes without sword-strokes. In your eyes, God of love! the murder of men is always loathsome. # Prayer

**I**nfinite God! — You have mercy upon all; for you are capable of all things, and you overlook the sins of men for the sake of repentance <note>This passage heavily paraphrases the Book of Wisdom 11:23–26, a deuterocanonical text emphasizing God's love for His creation.</note>. Merciful God! I thank you for the grace of your holy word; grant that I may be merciful, because my Savior was also merciful; that I may remember that every drink of water that I shall give to the least of your members <note>original: "Glieder" — a biblical reference to fellow believers as part of the "Body of Christ."</note> is given to you; that I reach out my hand to suffering virtue <note>original: "leidenden Tugend" — a phrase common in 18th-century devotion, referring to virtuous people who are experiencing poverty or hardship.</note>, and sow here the noble seed that will bear harvest in heaven; that through goodness and virtue, through teaching and example, through help and rescue, through warning and edification, through comfort and friendship, I may find mercy before you again, O Father of mankind! Grant that I may become like Jesus Christ in all things through my conduct <note>original: "Wandel" — refers to one’s way of life or daily moral behavior.</note>.

# Evening Prayer.

# Contemplation of Death
### and
# Prayer.

---

Why should I fear death, which leads me back into your arms, you God of love? — Should the child tremble when he enters the house of his father? Should the infant weep when its mother takes it to her breast? Are you not the God of love? The God of love, who hates none of his creatures? <note>This reflects a common Enlightenment-era theological view of a benevolent Creator rather than a vengeful judge.</note> And I should tremble when you beckon to me? O no, Father of mankind — — also my Father, whom my heart loves with confidence! I will not be afraid. You, who gave me so many joys of life; you, whose wisdom ordered everything for the well-being of the creatures; whose goodness rules even in destruction, and your love in the death of things. I do not cease to be; I step into a better world, come closer to you, cast off the sensory body <note>original: "sinnlichen Körper"; the physical, material body that perceives the world through the senses</note>, which separated me from you. Death is more horrific for the one who sees the dying person than for the one who dies. It is the end of suffering, it

judge You will appear, who demands an account of my life’s conduct.
