No, You are not such a judge; since You are the Judge of humankind, You do not cease to be their Father. Would I indeed be afraid if my father were to be judge over me? — And if I am guilty in Your eyes, are You not then just as merciful as You are just? I myself will accuse myself before You, will confess all my crimes that I have committed in this earthly shell <note>original: "irdischen Hülle"; a common theological metaphor for the human body as a temporary vessel for the soul.</note> before You, and You will forgive me as a father forgives his child who has gone astray through error. Are You not like the benevolent shepherd who runs through wildernesses, climbs over mountains, and scales rocks to bring the lost sheep back to his flock? And if this lost sheep now surrendered itself again, would the shepherd then strangle it? Or throw it to the wolves himself, so that it should become their prey? Oh, what a thought! How passionately, my God,

I

<note>Vocabulary from the original German text: Judge (Richter), Accountability (Rechenschaft), Father (Vater), Merciful (barmherzig), Just (gerecht), Crimes (Verbrechen), Shepherd (Hirte), Sheep (Schaaf).</note>

<summary>The author reflects on God's nature as a Judge, arguing that divine judgment is rooted in fatherly love and mercy rather than the petty cruelty of human courts. Using the parable of the Lost Sheep, the text expresses confidence in divine forgiveness for those who confess their failings.</summary>
<keywords>divine judgment, mercy, God as Father, repentance, Good Shepherd, forgiveness, human nature</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the sentence started on page 129, where the author was reflecting on their previous misconceptions of God. It marks a transition from viewing God as a vengeful human-like judge to embracing Him as a source of infinite, parental love.</meta>

were my concepts of You! For You are not like men, who are capable of hatred. O, how erroneously I have judged You since my youth! I believed that You were like men, who can take revenge and kill; hence my fear of death. O God of Love! <note>original: "Gott der Liebe"</note> forgive me that I misjudged You. You are Love: no human has ever loved as You love. The most perfect, purest love of man is but the smallest spark of Your love; recognition <term>Erkenntniß; here referring to spiritual insight or the true internalizing of God's nature</term> alone leads me back into Your arms, and chains my soul to You; it alone gives me pure, perfect repentance <term>Reue</term>, that I have loved so little, while You loved me infinitely. I die now gladly, if You will, and as You will; in pain, Your love will give me relief, in death, comfort; but if You still prolong <note>original: "fristest"; to grant a reprieve or extend a specific period of time</note> my life, then prolong it for me so that I may yet make amends for the mistakes I have committed out of a lack of pure love. Let me shape my heart and my soul as You wish for me to be down here; but if it is no longer Your will that I should live, then forgive the mortal his earthly sins, forget the sinner, and see in me only the child who returns to the arms of the most loving father, and pleads for forgiveness and pardon. Amen.

<summary>The author concludes a deeply personal prayer, repenting for their former fear of death and their misunderstanding of God's character. They surrender their fate to God, viewing Him no longer as a punisher but as a loving father.</summary>
<keywords>God of Love, spiritual insight, repentance, death, divine mercy, fatherhood of God, forgiveness</keywords>

### Fourth Psalm.

### Of God the Righteous Judge.

1) God the Father has given all power to His Son Jesus Christ, in heaven and on earth.

2) This [Son] will therefore one day judge everyone, the sinners and the righteous, and to each He will give what he has deserved, whether good or evil.

3) We can easily deceive other people through pretense <term>pretense: "Verstellung," the act of hiding one's true feelings or character</term> and lies; but God does not allow Himself to be deceived; — He knows all our thoughts! — He looks into the heart, and everything is laid bare before Him.

4) With Him there is no respect of persons <note>original: "kein Ansehen der Person." This is a biblical idiom meaning that God does not show favoritism or partiality based on a person's social status or wealth.</note>; the king and the beggar, the rich and the poor, are equal before His eyes.

5) He does not allow Himself to be blinded by the glitter of gold, nor by the splendor of houses and palaces, but He judges according to justice and truth.

6) Woe to those who have done wrong and have no longer made amends for it; — to His left 

<note>The text ends mid-sentence, likely referring to the biblical scene of the Last Judgment where the "goats" (the unrighteous) are placed on the left hand of the judge.</note>

O God! All good fortune and misfortune, everything that happens to me, happens because You ordain it or permit it. I recognize and submit myself to Your divine will. I worship You. May I continue and conclude my short earthly life with the joyful expectation of a better and eternal one, and may I laud and praise Your divine providence in heavenly glory forever. Amen.

<meta>This page continues the personal prayer to the Virgin Mary begun on the previous page. It takes the form of a meditative commentary on the "Hail Mary" (Ave Maria) prayer, breaking it down line by line to reflect on Mary's status and virtues.</meta>

### Hail Mary.
<note>original: "Gegrüßet seyst du Maria."</note>

I recognize and venerate you as a Mother of God, my Savior <term>Savior: From the German "Heiland," referring to Jesus Christ as the redeemer of humanity.</term>. I recognize and venerate you as my most effective advocate <term>Advocate: An intercessor; someone who pleads a case on behalf of another at the throne of God.</term> at the throne of my God, and I commend myself to your protection and your intercession.

### You are full of grace.
<note>original: "Du bist voll der Gnaden."</note>

God has filled you with His graces; He has kept you immaculate <term>Immaculate: Free from the stain of "original sin," a central Catholic doctrine regarding Mary's purity from her own conception.</term> and pure from all sin! He has adorned you with all the virtues and perfections that must be proper to a Mother of God.

### The Lord is with you.
<note>original: "Der Herr ist mit dir."</note>

He was always with you, and never had a sin separated you from Him. He was with you through your entire life with His protection and His grace; and He is with you still, and through all eternity, in the fullness of His glory and majesty.

### Blessed are you among women.
<note>original: "Du bist gebenedeyet unter den Weibern."</note>

God has blessed you, and from all of your kind <note>original: "deines Geschlechtes." In this context, it refers to the female sex or the human race as a whole.</note> has chosen you as the Mother of Him through whom all nations should be blessed.

<vocab>Mary, Mother of God, Savior, Advocate, Grace, Sin, Virtues, Perfections, Glory, Majesty, Blessed</vocab>

### And blessed is the fruit of your womb, Jesus.
<note>original: "Und gebenedeyet ist die Frucht deines Leibes, Jesus."</note>

Through him you were blessed; we bless <term>Bless: The German text uses "benedenen," an archaic form of "segnen" (to bless), derived from the Latin "benedicere."</term> him with you, and bless you for his sake. Blessed is the womb that bore him! Blessed are the breasts that nursed him! <note>This passage paraphrases Luke 11:27–28, where Jesus responds to a woman's praise of his mother by emphasizing that those who hear and keep the word of God are truly blessed.</note> Yet you are far more blessed, you who have always loved him, and heard him, and kept the word of God.

### Holy Mary, Mother of God.
<note>original: "Heilige Maria Mutter Gottes."</note>

You who conceived and gave birth to Jesus Christ, who is God; your intercession <term>Intercession: "Fürbitte," the act of praying to God or a divine figure on behalf of another person.</term> can achieve all things with your Son; it can achieve all things with the Almighty, and your goodness toward humanity equals your power in heaven.

### Pray for us.
<note>original: "Bitte für uns."</note>

Ask God on our behalf for all the graces <term>Graces: "Gnaden," referring to divine influence or favors granted by God to help a person lead a holy life.</term> we need to be pleasing to Him, and through your intercession, obtain for us access to Him. Through Jesus Christ your Son, our Savior <term>Savior: "Heiland," a traditional German title for Christ, literally meaning "the healer" or "the one who brings salvation."</term> and Redeemer <term>Redeemer: "Erlöser," referring to Christ as the one who pays the price to free humanity from sin.</term>.

### Poor sinners.
<note>original: "Arme Sünder."</note>

Because of our sins, we are unworthy to turn directly to God and to plead for the <term>Graces: "Gnaden," referring to divine influence or favors granted by God to help a person lead a holy life.</term> graces that are so indispensable to us. Ask on our behalf for the grace of true repentance and the forgiveness of our misdeeds.

### Now and in the hour of our death. Amen.
<note>original: "Jetzt und in der Stunde unsers Absterbens. Amen."</note>

Pray for us to receive the wisdom that enlightens the mind and instructs us in <term>Godliness: "Gottseligkeit," a state of being devout and living a life pleasing to God.</term> godliness. Ask for us an earnest, steadfast, and sincere piety; the grace to imitate your holy virtues; strengthen us in our faith, in our hope, and in our love, and stir up within us an insurmountable hatred for sin. Above all, however, ask for our perseverance in goodness until our very end, and the grace to die the death of the righteous—fully reconciled with God and strengthened by a worthy partaking of the most holy <term>Last Supper: "Abendmahl," the German term used here for the Eucharist or Holy Communion. In the context of the "hour of death," it refers to the Viaticum, the final reception of the sacrament.</term> Last Supper. Amen.

<meta>This page follows the conclusion of the line-by-line commentary on the "Hail Mary" prayer, transitioning into a formal prayer focused on the "Imitation of Mary," a common theme in Catholic devotional literature emphasizing the adoption of her virtues.</meta>

### Prayer
### for the Imitation of Mary.

Dearest Jesus! Make us like the example of your most blessed Mother, and above all, your most holy example, so that we here on earth may vividly feel the value of virtue and the happiness which it grants, and after this life become partakers of eternal bliss. Let Mary’s <term>Humility: "Demuth," a central virtue in the text, representing a modest view of one's own importance and total reliance on God.</term> humility and trust in God, her gentleness and love for humanity, her devotion to the will of the Most High, and the purity of her character serve as a model and encouragement for us. May our hearts and our consciences henceforth be pure, our <term>Conduct: "Wandel," literally "walk," referring to one's moral path or way of life.</term> conduct blameless, and our entire behavior recollected, modest, and humble. To faithfully follow God’s will, to do good in secret, to precisely fulfill the <term>Duties of our station: "Obliegenheiten unsres Standes," referring to the specific social and moral responsibilities one has based on their position in life (e.g., as a parent, worker, or citizen).</term> duties of our station, and to observe our obligations even when unnoticed and unrewarded—let this henceforth be our honor and our endeavor; let this be the result of our devotion to Mary; then we shall one day also share in her eternal glory. Amen.

<summary>A devotional prayer addressed to Jesus, asking for the grace to emulate the virtues of the Virgin Mary, such as humility, purity, and quiet devotion to duty.</summary>
<keywords>imitation of Mary, Jesus, virtue, humility, devotion, duties of station, character, prayer</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from a Catholic prayer focused on the Virgin Mary to a mystical, highly emotional prayer attributed to Elizabeth Rowe. Elizabeth Singer Rowe (1674–1737) was an English poet and devout writer whose work, particularly "Devout Exercises of the Heart," became immensely popular in Germany through various translations. Her writing represents a bridge between English Dissenting piety and German Pietism or mystical Catholicism.</meta>

# Prayer
### of
# Elizabeth Rowe.

Let my enraptured soul, O <term>Source of Love: "Quelle der Liebe," a frequent metaphor in mystical literature for God as the origin of all affection and existence.</term> source of love, draw eternally from your delightful streams! Let it sink into the depths, into the fullness of your joys, where all its infinite desires will be perfectly satisfied and lost in an eternal abundance. Do I have, O Beloved of my soul, any joy, even a single thought of <term>Bliss: "Glückseligkeit," a state of supreme happiness or spiritual beatitude.</term> bliss apart from you? Are my desires truly stilled by the fact that you have crowned my days with abundance and peace? Do I not place all joy, the entire comfort of my life, in your grace, in your love alone? What use to me is this vain, dream-like scene of transitory things? My thoughts soar far above the boundaries of those worlds! This world has no charms for me; I have died to all its empty glimmers. But how my soul languishes for you with fer- <note>The original German text ends mid-word with "brünsti-," likely intended to be "brünstiger Begierde" (fervent desire).</note>

<summary>A highly emotional and mystical prayer by the English writer Elizabeth Rowe, expressing a soul's intense longing to find total satisfaction in God's love rather than worldly comforts.</summary>
<keywords>Elizabeth Rowe, mysticism, soul, divine love, bliss, Pietism, devotional literature, longing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of a prayer by Elizabeth Rowe from page 168. The text transitions from an intense, mystical expression of longing for the divine to a more traditional prayer of gratitude for the gift of life on one's birthday.</meta>

...vent, unrestricted desires! For you all my powers awaken; whatever can awaken desires, whatever can delight, whatever can satisfy all wishes, is found in you alone, you infinite <term>Abyss: "Abgrund," a term often used in Christian mysticism to describe the unfathomable and bottomless nature of God's essence.</term> abyss of rapture and love! How my soul wanders about in these wide, immeasurable joys! How all thoughts within me grow dark except for yours! I forget myself, I forget everything except you, the most glorious, the eternal object of my preoccupation! You shall also remain imprinted upon my soul, present until death, and after death, through the duration of my immortal being, I will direct my attention only toward your majestic perfections.

### Prayer on a Birthday.

I thank you, my God, for the life that you gave me; this life is your gift, you have drawn me out of <term>Nothingness: "aus dem Nichts," referring to the theological concept of *creatio ex nihilo*, or creation out of nothing.</term> nothingness, and through your goodness I am here. Who led me on the paths of this <term>Pilgrimage: "Wanderschaft," a metaphor for the human life span as a journey toward a spiritual home.</term> pilgrimage, if not you? Who sustained <note>The German text ends mid-sentence; the likely intended meaning is "Who sustained me?" (Wer erhielt mich?).</note>

<meta>This page continues the "Prayer on a Birthday," which began on the previous page. The text completes a rhetorical question about God's lifelong protection and moves into a deeply personal reflection on divine mercy, forgiveness for sins, and the speaker's total dependence on God's love.</meta>

me until this very moment? Who gifted me so many joyful days? Who gave me happiness—who softened my suffering, who poured comfort into my soul when sorrow and misery oppressed me? And to whom were my tears more precious—those I wept in silence—than to you, who created me? You thought of me before I could even think, and you counted my tears before I even knew I was crying, and you gave me comfort before I even knew what comfort was. With <term>Paternal goodness: "Vatergüte," a recurring theme in 18th-century devotion emphasizing a close, familial relationship with the Divine.</term> paternal goodness you warned me often, and you shattered the <term>Iron fetters: "eisernen Fesseln," a traditional Pietist metaphor representing the heavy, restrictive nature of sin and worldly vice.</term> iron fetters with which sin bound me. Mercifully, you took the chains from me and beckoned me toward forgiveness, as a father and as a friend. I stumbled, and you lifted me up again; I sank, you rushed to save me; and when I left you, you sought me out yourself; and when I returned, your arm was open to me—your fatherly arm. Oh, how much I owe you! How wonderfully you have led me!—If you grant me life even longer, then also give me a heart that recognizes you with obedience and never leaves you. Take everything from me, my God! only not your love. Amen.

# Mercy.

## The Omnipresence of God.

Redeemer of mankind, when you wrestled with death, when your soul rose to your Father and dissolved in holy prayer, when your sweat and your blood flowed upon the earth—in that solemn hour, you made known that great truth which shall remain truth as long as the shell of the eternal soul is dust. You stood and spoke to the sleepers: <note>original: "Willig ist eure Seele, allein das Fleisch ist schwach." A reference to Jesus' words in the Garden of Gethsemane (Matthew 26:41); while usually translated as "the spirit is willing" in English Bibles, the author here uses "Seele" (soul).</note> Your soul is willing, but the flesh is weak.

This lot of finitude, this weight of the earth, is also felt by my soul whenever it seeks to rise toward God—toward God, the Infinite One.

# Reflection
### on the
# Wisdom of God.

All-benevolent God! When I reflect upon Your wisdom, Your love, how worthy of adoration You are to me! Deeply worshiping, I will consider who You are — Wisdom and Love! — Filled with wonder, I bow before You in the dust <note>This expression "in the dust" is a common historical trope used to express extreme humility and reverence before the Divine.</note>, finding no words to exalt You: only my heart, only my feeling reaches out to You — my Lord and my Father! Wherever I look, I see the works of Your love. Magnificently the sun rises in the firmament; majesty is its garment; its sight gladdens every living creature: and this sun is Your work, You God of glory and of strength!

The darkness of night, the starry host <note>German: *Sternenheer*. A poetic way of describing the vast, organized multitude of stars in the sky.</note> that shines above me, beckons me to this worship; — everywhere I look, You are there; everything proclaims Your existence, everything Your greatness, Your love; everywhere is the temple of Your divinity.

<note>Key terms in this section include: Wisdom of God (*Weisheit Gottes*), All-benevolent God (*Allgütiger Gott*), Sun (*Sonne*), Firmament (*Firmament*), Starry host (*Sternenheer*), and Divinity (*Gottheit*).</note>

The seed, the forest, the field, are the heralds of Your love. — And I — a human being — endowed with a body that Your hand so wonderfully prepared — a human being to whom You gave understanding and the ability to recognize You — a heart to love You. — — O you animating principle within me <note>German: *Belebendes*. # Thoughts
## on
## Redemption.

God! can my heart truly think this thought—think the thought of the highest love—the thought of redemption! — In the holy darkness I feel Your great work, without my understanding fathoming You; and how could the worm <note>A common 18th-century metaphor for human humility and frailty in the face of the Divine.</note> truly fathom You, he who does not even know the sun’s wonders, nor can determine its path and structure, and yet in the sunlight feels the blessing of its warmth? — No, soul, no! you cannot fathom the Divine, the high decree <note>German: *Schluss*. In this context, it refers to the divine resolution or "plan of salvation" decided by God.</note>, when Jesus gave Himself as a sacrifice—you can only perceive the Divinity of love. O human wisdom! you, who are foolishness in the eye of eternal wisdom, do not take from me the comfort that Christ bore my guilt in the death of the cross. How anxiously would I have to tremble, if God were not my Redeemer! No! this comfort, which faith gives me, no mocker shall <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the bottom of the page; it likely continues with "take from me."</note>

## Omnipresent Wisdom of God.

Eternal Light 1), illuminate the spirit which you created for the <term>knowledge of yourself</term> <note>original: "Erkenntniß." What is the spirit of mortal man when he wanders far from your light in the <term>valleys of death</term> <note>A common poetic and biblical metaphor for a state of spiritual ignorance or separation from God.</note> 2)? Literally "brain-spinnings," referring to delusions or deceptive figments of the imagination produced by human pride.</note> and grows dizzy upon the summits of earthly wisdom. Hidden God, whom all your creatures seek, how infin—

beings. In vain, the <term>cleverling</term> <note>original: "Klügling." Spirit of Creation! <note>The author refers to Genesis 1, 2, 3, and 4, which detail the creation of the heavens, the earth, and the early history of humanity.</note> Eternal union of **omnipotence** <note>original: "Allmacht." ## Remembrance of God’s Love.

---

You God of love! have mercy on us.
You Father of humanity! have mercy on us.
You benefactor of all creatures! have mercy on us.
You friend of mortals! have mercy on us.
You supporter of the wretched! have mercy on us.
Heaven and the world proclaim Your love to us.
The sun and the moon, which shine so magnificently above us, proclaim Your love to us.

### Prayer.

O You God of purest love! grant that we may recognize the greatness of Your love, make ourselves worthy of this Your love, and embrace our neighbor <note>original: "Nächsten," referring to any fellow human being, as in the biblical command to "love thy neighbor."</note> with the holy love that is Your commandment—through the fulfillment of which, You Love of all love! man can only worthily love You! Amen.

### Adoration, Humility, and Confidence.

---

My God! I can and am permitted to love you! Let me think this great thought, and send your light into my soul. May the holy flame of your love lift my spirit out of the prison of sensuality <note>original: "Kerker der Sinnlichkeit"; in this 18th-century context, "sensuality" refers to the limitations of the physical senses and the material world which were thought to "imprison" the soul.</note> toward you, and fill it with your divine fire. Separate me for a few moments from the fetters of the senses, so that my spirit may approach yours and worship you in spirit and in truth <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 4:24, "God is a Spirit: and they that worship him must worship him in spirit and in truth."</note>.

Let me, Infinite One! <note>original: "Unendlicher"; a common Enlightenment-era epithet for God, emphasizing God's boundlessness.</note> feel your presence; feel your holy existence; feel that I breathe, live, and am, through you. This heart that beats in my breast; the blood that flows in my veins, moves and flows through your goodness. Without you, my heart would stop beating, my blood would stop flowing, my spirit would cease to live. You are the life of all things; I live through you and could not exist without you.

<vocab>Adoration, Humility, Confidence, Sensuality, Infinite One, Existence, Goodness</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a deeply personal meditative prayer that explores the soul's total dependence on God for both physical life and spiritual light. It encourages the reader to transcend the material world to find a direct, "truthful" connection with the Divine.</summary>

You are the Unity, the Power of all powers, through which everything acts: work in me the work of sanctification! Elevate my spirit, so that it may feel that it is approaching you. Distance from you is death; approach to you is life; union with you is eternal bliss <note>original: "Seligkeit"; a term implying supreme spiritual happiness or the state of being saved.</note>. # Closing Prayer.
### Elevation of his Spirit to the Creator.

---

Lord! what is the life of man, and what are his years? They flow away like a brook, and ripple away like a spring, and they are remembered no more.

Half of my pilgrimage <note>The German "Wanderschaft" implies a journey or a period of wandering, often used in a spiritual sense to describe earthly life as a temporary trek toward heaven.</note> and life's course is perhaps completed. — Perhaps I am closer to the goal than it appears. Lord! crown me when I have finished my race! <note>This imagery evokes the "crown of righteousness" mentioned in 2 Timothy 4:7-8, symbolizing the reward for a life of faith.</note> Speak to my soul: Go forth in peace! Speak to this earthly shell: Rest softly in the womb from which you emerged! <note>The "earthly shell" (irdische Hülle) is a poetic term for the physical body, while the "womb" (Schooße) here refers to the earth or the original state of nature.</note> Lord, take my soul up to you! Let the spirit return to where it came from.

Already so many times, Lord, you have let the sun rise over me in its full splendor. What was I, indeed, before you called me forth into life from the depths of eternal night? <note>"Eternal night" (ewigen Nacht) refers to the void or the state of non-existence prior to creation.</note>

What was I so many fleeting years ago? and who will I perhaps be shortly? Will I still, wrapped in this earthly shroud <note>The German "irdischen Decke" (earthly cover/shroud) refers to the physical body as a temporary garment for the soul.</note>, admire your glorious <term>omnipotence: the quality of having unlimited or very great power, specifically of God</term> in all creatures, and will I still gaze upon that spark of your radiance, the earthly sun? Or will my bones rest, gathered with the ashes of my ancestors? But no: I shall not die; for the thinking spirit within me shall live and proclaim the works of the Lord, even when my limbs have turned to dust.

I shall live—but how?—O Father of all created beings, have mercy on your creature! You said to me: "Be!" and I was. My eyes opened to the earthly light. Still too immature to think, my soul did not yet recognize its Creator. Unaccustomed to existence, I greeted the world with tears. I became a man, Lord! and I worshipped you.

### For the <term>Credo: from the Latin word meaning "I believe," this is the Nicene or Apostles' Creed set to music, where the congregation declares their core religious beliefs</term>.

# Hymns
## at a High Mass <term>High Mass: A solemn form of the Catholic liturgy, typically featuring a choir, incense, and a priest accompanied by a deacon and subdeacon.</term>

---

### For the <term>Introit: The opening chant or hymn of the Mass, performed as the priest approaches the altar.</term>

->1.<-

We cast ourselves down,
Before you, God of Sabaoth! <note>original: "Gott Sabaoth." A Hebrew title meaning "Lord of Hosts," emphasizing God’s majesty over the heavenly armies.</note>
Hear our songs,
As we, according to the commandment,
Bring this sacrifice <note>In the Catholic tradition, the Mass is referred to as the "Sacrifice of the Altar."</note> to you:
Grant now, that we
May sing of it with devotion,
And find favor with you.

->2.<-

The day before Jesus’ suffering
At the Last Supper,
As he wished to depart
From this valley of sorrows, <term>Valley of sorrows: Translated from the German "Jammerthal," a traditional poetic way of describing earthly life as a place of hardship and trial compared to the peace of heaven.</term>
He broke the bread,
And distributed the wine,
Blessed it and spoke,
"Do this, and think of me."

## For the Offertory. <note>original: "Zum Offertorium." The Offertory is the point in the Mass when the bread and wine are prepared and offered to God before the Consecration.</note>

->1.<-

> Lord! let these gifts,
> ->Be well-pleasing to You,<-
> Which we have offered up;
> ->Though they are indeed bread and wine;<-
> Yet they shall be transformed
> ->Into Christ’s flesh and blood;<- <note>This refers to the doctrine of Transubstantiation, the belief that the substance of the bread and wine changes into the actual body and blood of Christ.</note>
> That is for us here on earth
> ->And for the departed, good.<- <note>A reference to the traditional belief that the merits of the Mass can be applied to the souls of the deceased.</note>

->2.<-

> We offer up our will to You,
> ->Our heart and our thoughts,<-
> Help us so that we may fulfill
> ->In the course of our lives,<-
> What You have commanded us,
> ->Your children, to do.<-
> Take away what may hinder us,
> ->Take away the burden of sin.<-

->3.<-

> We lay at Your feet
> ->Our possessions and goods as well,<-
> And what we enjoy here,
> ->Our life, body, and blood.<- <note>original: "Leben, Leib und Blut." This indicates a total consecration of the self—physical and spiritual—mirroring the sacrifice on the altar.</note>

### After the Consecration. <note>original: "Wandlung." In the Catholic Mass, this refers to Transubstantiation, the moment the bread and wine are believed to become the body and blood of Christ.</note>

### 1.

Here I pray on my knees,
->Adoring You, hidden God!<- <note>original: "Verborgener Gott." This hymn is a German paraphrase of the Latin "Adoro Te Devote," traditionally attributed to St. Thomas Aquinas.</note>
I will not strive
->To understand what You have done here<-
By means of the senses;
->Your word alone must be,<-
To keep from straying here,
->The foundation of my faith.<-

### 2.

The Divinity was veiled
->Only on the altar of the Cross,<-
But here even
->The humanity is hidden as well.<- <note>This reflects the doctrine that while only Christ's divinity was hidden during the Crucifixion, both his divine and human natures are hidden under the "accidents" (outward appearance) of bread and wine in the Eucharist.</note>
This monument of Your goodness,
->This true Bread of Heaven,<-
Reminds my soul,
->O Lord, of Your death.<-

### 3.

Wash me from my sins,
->O Jesus, through Your blood,<- <note>original: "Jesu," the Latin vocative form of Jesus commonly used in German spiritual texts of this period.</note>
And let me find grace,
->You, the highest good.<-

### For the Agnus Dei. <note>original: "Zum Agnus Dei." The *Agnus Dei* (Lamb of God) is a liturgical prayer or chant in the Mass, traditionally recited or sung after the Consecration and before Communion.</note>

->1.<-

> Rejoice, you pious souls!
> A miracle has happened,
> The Lord wishes to hide himself;
> No eye can see Him.
> Under the forms of bread and wine <note>original: "Brods- und Weinsgestalten." In Catholic theology, these are the "accidents" or outward appearances of the elements that remain after the substance has changed.</note>
> Is Jesus' flesh and blood,
> Contained upon the altar,
> This greatest treasure of the soul. <note>original: "Seelengut," literally "soul-good" or spiritual benefit.</note>

->2.<-

> Concealed here is to be found
> The true Lamb of God, <note>original: "Gotteslamm." This identifies Jesus with the Passover lamb whose sacrifice brings salvation.</note>
> Who has blotted out the sins of all mankind
> Upon the wood of the cross.
> It is the food of souls,
> As faith teaches us;
> It nourishes us on our journey, <note>original: "Reise," referring to the "viaticum" or the journey of life toward eternity.</note>
> And yet is not consumed. <note>A reference to the theological concept that while the host is consumed, Christ remains whole and living.</note>

<term>Agnus Dei</term> (Lamb of God), <term>Gotteslamm</term> (Lamb of God), <term>Kreuzesstamm</term> (the wood/trunk of the Cross), <term>Seelengut</term> (soul-treasure/spiritual blessing), <term>Brods- und Weinsgestalten</term> (appearances of bread and wine).

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of a hymn to Christ's presence and the beginning of a section for the Agnus Dei. The verses emphasize the "miracle" of the Eucharist where Christ is present but hidden under the forms of bread and wine to nourish the believer's soul.</summary>
<keywords>Agnus Dei, Lamb of God, Eucharist, Transubstantiation, Real Presence, Altar, Soul's Journey</keywords>

# Other Mass Prayers.

### Introit. <note>original: "Introitus." The opening chant or prayer of the Mass as the priest approaches the altar.</note>

Lord! members of your Church

->We plead to you, O God!<-
We praise you, and offer
->Our sacrifice to you;<- <note>original: "Opfer." In this context, it refers to the "Holy Sacrifice of the Mass" as well as the prayers of the people.</note>

->Acknowledge your servants.<-

### Gloria. <note>The "Gloria in Excelsis Deo," a hymn of praise to the Holy Trinity.</note>

->Brought by your people!<-
Only you, Lord! are the Highest;
->Son of God, Jesus Christ!<-
You who are at the same time the closest to us,
->And are our Lord and God!<- <note>original: "der Nächste." A poetic contrast highlighting Christ's divinity as the "Highest" and his humanity/presence as our "Neighbor" or the one nearest to us.</note>

->Through the mouths of the prophets,<-

### Prayer
### after the holy Mass.

**D**earest God! I thank you with all my heart that you have granted me the grace to attend this holy sacrifice. <note>original: "Opfer." In Catholic theology, the Mass is referred to as a "sacrifice" because it is a memorial and re-presentation of Christ's sacrifice on the cross.</note> Grant me also to be worthy of the enviable benefit that fervent Christians draw from it. — If I have not attended this sacrifice with that devotion which such a holy act deserved; then forgive me these shortcomings, for the sake of the merits of my Redeemer, <note>original: "Erlöser." A title for Jesus Christ, emphasizing his role in "redeeming" or buying back humanity from sin.</note> who offered himself to you in the same, and grant that I may actively improve this fault in the future. Amen.

# Hymn
## for the Festival of the Resurrection.

->Melody according to the Latin hymn:<-
->*The Morning Sky Purples.*<- <note>original: "Aurora Coelum purpurat." A traditional Latin hymn for Lauds (morning prayer) during the Easter season, often attributed to St. Ambrose, celebrating the dawn of the Resurrection.</note>

### 1.

Rejoice greatly, O Christendom!
Let triumph and the joy of victory resound!
The Savior <term>Heiland</term> <note>Heiland: A traditional German term for "Savior" or "Redeemer," derived from the word for "healing" or "making whole."</note> has, with God's power,
Arisen from the night of the grave.

### 2.

Oh! Gloriously it is now revealed to us,
And everything so precisely fulfilled,
Which the Scripture once foretold;
The salvation of the world is now accomplished.

### 3.

Welcome, great day of victory!
You took away the Savior’s shame of death,
You have highly glorified innocence,
And eternally proven God’s teaching.

->4.<-

Who would not hold her in high esteem?
->Since she was the one full of grace,<- <note>original: "Gnadenvolle." This is the German equivalent of the Latin *Gratia plena* from the "Hail Mary" prayer (Luke 1:28).</note>
Who for us—oh, feel it with tears of joy—
->Once gave birth to the Redeemer of the world.<-
With delight our spirit bows
To you, sublime Mother!

---

->5.<-

When the breath of death wafted through the world,
And the curse still weighed heavily upon us,
Then she arrived like the dawn <note>original: "Morgenröthe." In Christian art and literature, Mary is frequently symbolized as the Aurora or dawn that precedes the "Sun of Righteousness," Jesus.</note>
->Preceding the Sun of Life for us.<-
She has brought the gentle day
To us, after the horrific night of sin.

---

->6.<-

You walked modestly through life
->In quiet seclusion;<- <note>original: "Eingezogenheit." A term used to describe a life of inward contemplation and withdrawal from the distractions of the world.</note>
A gentle heart was given to you,
->Heaven’s peace and soft joy;<-

<term>Immaculate</term> (Unbefleckte), <term>Full of Grace</term> (Gnadenvolle), <term>Redeemer of the World</term> (Welterlöser), <term>Dawn</term> (Morgenröthe), <term>Sin</term> (Sünde), <term>Night of Horror</term> (Schauernacht), <term>Seclusion</term> (Eingezogenheit)

<summary>This page of the hymn celebrates Mary’s role in the Nativity and her symbolic position as the "dawn" that ends the night of sin. It portrays her as a figure of both cosmic importance and personal, quiet humility.</summary>
<keywords>Virgin Mary, Redeemer, Salvation history, Dawn metaphor, Grace, Humility, Nativity</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Hymn to Mary" from page 281. The poem continues to enumerate the virtues of the Virgin Mary, moving from her role in salvation history to her personal qualities of faith, devotion to God’s word, and her domestic life.</meta>

With love our spirit bows
To you, sublime pilgrim. <note>original: "Pilgerin." In Christian tradition, earthly life is often described as a pilgrimage, and Mary is viewed here as the foremost traveler on the path of faith.</note>

---

->7.<-

Full of faith you looked without wavering
Upon your rough path of life,
And spoke in your bitter days:
What God does, that is well done. <note>original: "Was Gott thut, das ist wohlgethan." This is a direct reference to a famous 1674 hymn by Samuel Rodigast, which became a standard expression of Christian resignation to God's will.</note>
With reverence our spirit bows
To you, you Queen of Virtue.

---

->8.<-

The word of the Most High was dear to you,
Your highest treasure in this world.
Who loved more honestly, who more faithfully
The God who keeps faith and promise?
With devotion our spirit bows
To you, you God-devoted one! <note>original: "Gottergeb'ne." A term describing a soul completely surrendered and yielded to the divine will.</note>

---

->9.<-

In your dwelling love reigned
And quiet, happy domesticity;
The pious maternal impulses,
They were always your garment of honor.
With warmth our spirit bows
To you, you good mother!

<term>Faith</term> (Glauben), <term>Path of Life</term> (Lebensbahn), <term>Queen of Virtue</term> (Tugend-Königin), <term>God</term> (Gott), <term>Devotion</term> (Andacht), <term>Domesticity</term> (Häuslichkeit), <term>Mother</term> (Mutter)

<summary>This page concludes the descriptive stanzas of the hymn, focusing on Mary's endurance through hardship, her devotion to scripture, and her role as the ideal model of domestic and maternal virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Virgin Mary, faith, providence, domesticity, maternal love, Christian virtue, pilgrimage of life</keywords>

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Hymn to Mary" from page 282. Having previously described her earthly virtues, the hymn now shifts its focus to Mary's current state in heaven and her role as an intercessor for believers on earth.</meta>

### 10.

> There you shine like the sun of heaven
> ->At God’s throne with glory,<-
> And look down with maternal bliss
> ->Now upon all of Christendom.<-
> Reverently our spirit bows,
> To you, crowned victress. <note>original: "Siegerin." In a religious context, this describes Mary as one who has triumphed over earthly suffering and stands victorious in heaven.</note>

---

### 11.

> When sorrow and suffering plague us,
> ->Your example shall be our guide:<- <note>original: "Nachfolg'." Literally "following" or "succession," implying that the believer should model their behavior after hers.</note>
> That we, like you, in days of mourning
> ->May also rejoice even among thorns.<- <note>A poetic reference to finding spiritual joy or peace even in the midst of painful circumstances, often alluding to the "crown of thorns."</note>
> We fix our gaze upon you
> Trustfully in misfortune.

---

### 12.

> Mary, most highly blessed! <note>original: "Hochgebenedeyte." This is the traditional German translation of the Latin *benedicta*, used in the "Hail Mary" prayer: "Blessed art thou among women."</note>
> ->Be our gentle comforter;<-
> Be now, and once in the struggle of death
> ->Our mediatrix before God.<- <note>original: "Mittlerin." A theological term for Mary’s role as an intercessor who brings the prayers of the faithful to God.</note>
> Pray, that one day in the judgment
> Your Son may speak a joyful verdict.

<vocab>Mary, Christendom, God’s throne, Victress, Comforter, Mediatrix, Judgment</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the hymn by depicting Mary in her heavenly glory and asking for her intercession and comfort, particularly at the hour of death and during the final judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Virgin Mary, Heaven, Intercession, Mediatrix, Christian life, Suffering, Judgment, Salvation</keywords>

--- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Hymn to Mary" that began on page 282. Stanzas 10 through 12 on the previous page transitioned from Mary's virtues to her role as a heavenly intercessor. This final stanza serves as a doxology—a short hymn of praise to the Holy Trinity.</meta>

### 13.

->Let us highly exalt the Father;<-
->Praise be to Him, honor, and glory!<-
->Who gave His eternal Son<-
->A mother here in the realm of time.<- <note>original: "in der Zeit." This phrase highlights the theological concept of the Incarnation, where the eternal Son of God entered the temporal, human world through a human mother.</note>
->Praise, all tongues, praise and laud<-
->The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.<-

---

<vocab>Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Praise, Honor, Glory, Mother</vocab>

<summary>This page contains the final stanza of a hymn dedicated to Mary, concluding with a traditional doxology that praises the Trinity for the gift of the Incarnation. It emphasizes the connection between the eternal God and the temporal world through Mary's motherhood.</summary>

<keywords>Doxology, Trinity, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Incarnation, Praise, Motherhood</keywords>
